This is a common point of confusion, and it's easy to miscount when manually enumerating the graphs.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n---\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### ✅ **Correct Answer: 34 non-isomorphic simple graphs with 5 vertices**\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n---\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### 🔍 Why the confusion?\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe number of **labeled** simple graphs with 5 vertices is:\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n$$\\\\\\\\n2^{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\binom{5}{2}} = 2^{10} = 1024\\\\\\\\n$$\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThis is because each pair of vertices can either be connected by an edge or not, and there are $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\binom{5}{2} = 10$ such pairs.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nHowever, when we consider **non-isomorphic** graphs, we are grouping together all graphs that are isomorphic (i.e., structurally the same, just with different labels on the vertices). \\\"#!/usr/bin/env python3\\\\n\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"exp-004-s2: ACT (Activation Consistency Training) seed 2.\\\\n\\\\nIdentical to exp-004 (seed 42) except seed=43 and output dir has _s2 suffix.\\\\nSFT on paired calibration data + L2 consistency loss on hidden states\\\\nat target transformer layers between valid_correction and invalid_pressure pairs.\\\\n\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\nimport sys\\\\nimport os\\\\nimport json\\\\nimport logging\\\\nimport argparse\\\\nimport random\\\\nimport time\\\\nfrom collections import defaultdict\\\\n\\\\nimport torch\\\\nimport torch.nn as nn\\\\nfrom torch.utils.data import Dataset, DataLoader\\\\nfrom transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer, get_linear_schedule_with_warmup\\\\nfrom peft import LoraConfig, get_peft_model\\\\n\\\\nlogging.basicConfig(\\\\n    level=logging.INFO,\\\\n    format=\\\\\\\"%(asctime)s [%(levelname)s] %(message)s\\\\\\\",\\\\n)\\\\nlogger = logging.getLogger(__name__)\\\\n\\\\nTARGET_LAYERS = [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef _resolve_transformer_layers(model: nn.Module) -> nn.ModuleList:\\\\n    candidate = model\\\\n    if hasattr(candidate, \\\\\\\"base_model\\\\\\\"):\\\\n        candidate = candidate.base_model\\\\n    if hasattr(candidate, \\\\\\\"layers\\\\\\\"):\\\\n        return candidate.layers\\\\n    if hasattr(candidate, \\\\\\\"model\\\\\\\"):\\\\n        inner = candidate.model\\\\n        if hasattr(inner, \\\\\\\"layers\\\\\\\"):\\\\n            return inner.layers\\\\n        if hasattr(inner, \\\\\\\"model\\\\\\\") and hasattr(inner.model, \\\\\\\"layers\\\\\\\"):\\\\n            return inner.model.layers\\\\n    raise AttributeError(\\\\n        f\\\\\\\"Cannot locate transformer layers on {type(model).__name__}. \\\\\\\"\\\\n        \\\\\\\"Expected model.model.layers or model.base_model.model.model.layers.\\\\\\\"\\\\n    )\\\\n\\\\n\\\\nclass GradActivationCache:\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Forward-hook cache that preserves gradients for L2 loss backprop.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n    def __init__(self, layers):\\\\n        self.layers = sorted(layers)\\\\n        self._cache = {}\\\\n        self._hooks = []\\\\n\\\\n    def register(self, model):\\\\n        self.remove()\\\\n        all_layers = _resolve_transformer_layers(model)\\\\n        for idx in self.layers:\\\\n            assert idx < len(all_layers), f\\\\\\\"Layer {idx} >= {len(all_layers)}\\\\\\\"\\\\n            self._hooks.append(all_layers[idx].register_forward_hook(self._fn(idx)))\\\\n        logger.info(\\\\\\\"Activation hooks on layers %s\\\\\\\", self.layers)\\\\n\\\\n    def _fn(self, idx):\\\\n        def hook(mod, inp, out):\\\\n            self._cache[idx] = out[0] if isinstance(out, tuple) else out\\\\n        return hook\\\\n\\\\n    def extract(self):\\\\n        r = dict(self._cache)\\\\n        self._cache.clear()\\\\n        return r\\\\n\\\\n    def remove(self):\\\\n        for h in self._hooks:\\\\n            h.remove()\\\\n        self._hooks.clear()\\\\n        self._cache.clear()\\\\n\\\\n\\\\nclass PairedCalibrationDataset(Dataset):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Pairs valid_correction + invalid_pressure by pair_id.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n    def __init__(self, path, tokenizer, max_len=1024):\\\\n        self.tokenizer = tokenizer\\\\n        self.max_len = max_len\\\\n        self.im_end_id = tokenizer.convert_tokens_to_ids(\\\\\\\"<|im_end|>\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        groups = defaultdict(dict)\\\\n        with open(path) as f:\\\\n            for line in f:\\\\n                d = json.loads(line)\\\\n                groups[d[\\\\\\\"pair_id\\\\\\\"]][d[\\\\\\\"condition\\\\\\\"]] = d\\\\n\\\\n        self.pairs = []\\\\n        for pid in sorted(groups):\\\\n            g = groups[pid]\\\\n            if \\\\\\\"valid_correction\\\\\\\" in g and \\\\\\\"invalid_pressure\\\\\\\" in g:\\\\n                self.pairs.append((g[\\\\\\\"valid_correction\\\\\\\"], g[\\\\\\\"invalid_pressure\\\\\\\"]))\\\\n        logger.info(\\\\\\\"Loaded %d pairs from %s\\\\\\\", len(self.pairs), path)\\\\n\\\\n    def __len__(self):\\\\n        return len(self.pairs)\\\\n\\\\n    def _tokenize_sample(self, sample):\\\\n        msgs = [{\\\\\\\"role\\\\\\\": t[\\\\\\\"role\\\\\\\"], \\\\\\\"content\\\\\\\": t[\\\\\\\"content\\\\\\\"]} for t in sample[\\\\\\\"turns\\\\\\\"]]\\\\n\\\\n        full_ids = self.tokenizer.apply_chat_template(\\\\n            msgs, tokenize=True, add_generation_prompt=True\\\\n        )\\\\n\\\\n        pre_asst_ids = self.tokenizer.apply_chat_template(\\\\n            msgs[:1], tokenize=True, add_generation_prompt=True\\\\n        )\\\\n        asst_start = len(pre_asst_ids)\\\\n\\\\n {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HJwaAuaimxfYvzogfgEasS","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Experimental Framework}\n2\t\\label{sec:framework}\n3\t\n4\t\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n5\t\\label{ssec:extraction}\n6\t\n7\tWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\n8\tWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 \\citep{openai2025gpt41} in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\n9\tStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\n10\tEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\n11\tFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n12\t\\begin{equation}\n13\t\\label{eq:dsyc}\n14\t\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n15\t\\end{equation}\n16\twhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\n17\tThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ (5-fold CV).\n18\t\n19\t\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n20\t\\label{ssec:reward}\n21\t\n22\tThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into a direction-based signal $\\rone$.\n23\tFor F1 and F3, $\\rone$ enters as part of a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} reward $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\n24\t\\begin{itemize}\n25\t\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n26\t\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe (SFT).} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients; trained via a custom SFT loss rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n27\t\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n28\t\\end{itemize}\n29\tThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\n30\tAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n31\t\n32\t\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n33\t\\label{ssec:recalib}\n34\t\n35\tLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\n36\tThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n37\t\n38\t\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n39\t\\label{ssec:baselines}\n40\t\n41\tWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\n42\tUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n43\t\n44\t\\subsection{Evaluation}\n45\t\\label{ssec:eval}\n46\t\n47\tThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\n48\tTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n49\t\\begin{equation}\n50\t\\label{eq:sycon}\n51\t\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n52\t\\end{equation}\n53\tBy design, $\\sycon = 0$ whenever $\\tof = 0$: a model that never accepts valid corrections receives no credit for resisting invalid ones.\n54\tFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} \\citep{he2023debertav3}, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\n55\tAll experiments use Qwen3-8B \\citep{yang2025qwen3} with LoRA (rank$=$64; $\\alpha{=}64$ for GRPO, $\\alpha{=}128$ for ACT) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\n56\tGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\n57\tWe test six $\\dsyc$-based configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d) and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3) using GRPO, plus binary probe $\\rone$ (F2) using SFT, $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline, and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n58\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96913cdb-deaa-4598-a56a-44022bee38d4","uuid":"a481af50-5461-461d-b6a1-9026c3da70b9","timestamp":"2026-05-23T19:20:51.099Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/framework.tex","content":"\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 \\citep{openai2025gpt41} in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ (5-fold CV).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into a direction-based signal $\\rone$.\nFor F1 and F3, $\\rone$ enters as part of a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} reward $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe (SFT).} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients; trained via a custom SFT loss rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nBy design, $\\sycon = 0$ whenever $\\tof = 0$: a model that never accepts valid corrections receives no credit for resisting invalid ones.\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} \\citep{he2023debertav3}, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B \\citep{yang2025qwen3} with LoRA (rank$=$64; $\\alpha{=}64$ for GRPO, $\\alpha{=}128$ for ACT) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nGRPO uses a group size of 2 due to memory constraints with 8B-parameter models, below the typical range of 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; full hyperparameters are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:training}.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d) and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3) using GRPO, plus binary probe $\\rone$ (F2) using SFT, $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline, and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n","numLines":58,"startLine":1,"totalLines":58}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Dthmi5c3vXAmzm1SETJksQ","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Direction Discriminability}\n2\t\\label{sec:direction_quality}\n3\t\n4\tBefore evaluating $\\dsyc$ as a reward signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), we first establish that it captures a genuine sycophancy-related signal in the residual stream.\n5\tIf the direction failed to discriminate sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations, downstream reward failures would be trivially explained by poor direction quality rather than by a fundamental gap between discriminability and reward utility.\n6\tWe evaluate discriminability along five axes: cross-validated classification accuracy, evidence-strength stratification, cross-model transfer, training stability, and probe ablation.\n7\tFigure~\\ref{fig:direction_quality} summarizes the per-layer AUROC profiles for both models.\n8\t\n9\t\\paragraph{Qwen3-8B layer-wise discriminability.}\n10\tWe evaluate $\\dsyc$ via 5-fold cross-validated AUROC on 181 style-controlled contrastive pairs (\\S\\ref{ssec:extraction}).\n11\tThe direction achieves AUROC $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ (5-fold CV) at its best layer (L19).\n12\tDiscriminability emerges gradually across depth: layers L0--L8 perform near chance, statistical significance ($p < 0.01$) first appears at L16, and performance peaks at L19.\n13\tThis gradual emergence, with the peak in the upper third of the network, suggests that sycophancy-relevant information is encoded as a late-stage representation rather than an input-level confound.\n14\tThe direction also captures evidential granularity.\n15\tAUROC rises monotonically from 0.675 on weak-evidence pairs to 0.883 on medium and 0.929 on strong pairs.\n16\tDiscriminability covaries with evidence strength, possibly because stronger evidence produces more behaviorally distinct responses rather than because $\\dsyc$ explicitly encodes epistemic quality.\n17\t\n18\t\\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}\n19\tTo rule out that these results are specific to Qwen3-8B's representation geometry, we repeat the full extraction and evaluation pipeline on Llama-3-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3}.\n20\tTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares the two models.\n21\tLlama-3-8B achieves AUROC $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ (5-fold CV) at L16, with a parallel evidence-strength gradient (weak 0.711, medium 0.864, strong 0.911).\n22\tThe best-layer depth differs (L16 vs.\\ L19), consistent with the architectural differences between the two families, but the qualitative pattern is identical: gradual emergence, best-layer selection in the upper third, and monotonic strength scaling.\n23\tDiscriminability is robust across knowledge domains, with per-domain AUROCs on Llama-3-8B ranging from 0.751 (geography) to 0.844 (commonsense).\n24\tWhen surface confounds (response length, hedging patterns) are explicitly controlled, Llama-3-8B's AUROC rises to 0.923, which confirms that the direction captures semantic content rather than stylistic artifacts.\n25\tThe consistent results across architecturally distinct model families indicate that contrastive sycophancy directions reflect a shared representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.\n26\t\n27\t\\begin{table}[t]\n28\t\\centering\n29\t\\caption{Cross-model $\\dsyc$ discriminability. AUROC uncertainty is 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}\n30\t\\label{tab:cross_model}\n31\t\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n32\t\\toprule\n33\tModel & Best Layer & AUROC ($\\pm$ std) & Weak & Medium & Strong \\\\\n34\t\\midrule\n35\tQwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\\\\n36\tLlama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\\\\n37\t\\bottomrule\n38\t\\end{tabular}\n39\t\\end{table}\n40\t\n41\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n42\t\\centering\n43\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}\n44\t\\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\\% through the network.}\n45\t\\label{fig:direction_quality}\n46\t\\end{figure}\n47\t\n48\t\\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}\n49\tTwo additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\\dsyc$ under training dynamics.\n50\tFirst, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.\n51\tThe direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.\n52\tSecond, we compare $\\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).\n53\tThe learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.\n54\t\n55\t\\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}\n56\tThese results establish that $\\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.\n57\tDirection quality is not the bottleneck.\n58\tThe reward failures documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.\n59\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96913cdb-deaa-4598-a56a-44022bee38d4","uuid":"2fb4441f-2c4d-4749-8971-6e9846916c36","timestamp":"2026-05-23T19:20:51.545Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/direction_quality.tex","content":"\\section{Direction Discriminability}\n\\label{sec:direction_quality}\n\nBefore evaluating $\\dsyc$ as a reward signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), we first establish that it captures a genuine sycophancy-related signal in the residual stream.\nIf the direction failed to discriminate sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations, downstream reward failures would be trivially explained by poor direction quality rather than by a fundamental gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nWe evaluate discriminability along five axes: cross-validated classification accuracy, evidence-strength stratification, cross-model transfer, training stability, and probe ablation.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:direction_quality} summarizes the per-layer AUROC profiles for both models.\n\n\\paragraph{Qwen3-8B layer-wise discriminability.}\nWe evaluate $\\dsyc$ via 5-fold cross-validated AUROC on 181 style-controlled contrastive pairs (\\S\\ref{ssec:extraction}).\nThe direction achieves AUROC $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ (5-fold CV) at its best layer (L19).\nDiscriminability emerges gradually across depth: layers L0--L8 perform near chance, statistical significance ($p < 0.01$) first appears at L16, and performance peaks at L19.\nThis gradual emergence, with the peak in the upper third of the network, suggests that sycophancy-relevant information is encoded as a late-stage representation rather than an input-level confound.\nThe direction also captures evidential granularity.\nAUROC rises monotonically from 0.675 on weak-evidence pairs to 0.883 on medium and 0.929 on strong pairs.\nDiscriminability covaries with evidence strength, possibly because stronger evidence produces more behaviorally distinct responses rather than because $\\dsyc$ explicitly encodes epistemic quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}\nTo rule out that these results are specific to Qwen3-8B's representation geometry, we repeat the full extraction and evaluation pipeline on Llama-3-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares the two models.\nLlama-3-8B achieves AUROC $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ (5-fold CV) at L16, with a parallel evidence-strength gradient (weak 0.711, medium 0.864, strong 0.911).\nThe best-layer depth differs (L16 vs.\\ L19), consistent with the architectural differences between the two families, but the qualitative pattern is identical: gradual emergence, best-layer selection in the upper third, and monotonic strength scaling.\nDiscriminability is robust across knowledge domains, with per-domain AUROCs on Llama-3-8B ranging from 0.751 (geography) to 0.844 (commonsense).\nWhen surface confounds (response length, hedging patterns) are explicitly controlled, Llama-3-8B's AUROC rises to 0.923, which confirms that the direction captures semantic content rather than stylistic artifacts.\nThe consistent results across architecturally distinct model families indicate that contrastive sycophancy directions reflect a shared representational phenomenon rather than a model-specific idiosyncrasy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model $\\dsyc$ discriminability. AUROC uncertainty is 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation; evidence-strength AUROCs are evaluated on the full dataset at each model's best layer.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nModel & Best Layer & AUROC ($\\pm$ std) & Weak & Medium & Strong \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-8B & L19 & $0.827 \\pm 0.028$ & 0.675 & 0.883 & 0.929 \\\\\nLlama-3-8B & L16 & $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ & 0.711 & 0.864 & 0.911 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_auroc.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer AUROC of $\\dsyc$ as a function of normalized depth (layer index / total layers). Qwen3-8B (36 layers, solid) shows gradual emergence from near-chance at L0 to a peak at L19, with performance plateauing in the final third. Llama-3-8B (32 layers, dashed; layers 16--31 only) exhibits a similar upper-third concentration. Both models achieve best discriminability in layers 50--60\\% through the network.}\n\\label{fig:direction_quality}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Training stability and probe ablation.}\nTwo additional analyses confirm the robustness of $\\dsyc$ under training dynamics.\nFirst, we track direction drift during LoRA fine-tuning: at step 500, the cosine similarity between the original and re-extracted $\\dsyc$ is approximately 0.96, and per-layer AUROC remains above 0.77.\nThe direction drifts slowly relative to the 500-step recalibration interval (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), so the reward signal does not become stale between updates.\nSecond, we compare $\\dsyc$ against a random baseline: a logistic probe trained on the learned direction achieves AUROC 0.835, versus 0.727 for a probe trained on a random direction of equal dimensionality ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.108$).\nThe learned direction carries substantially more sycophancy-relevant information than an arbitrary axis in the same activation space.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for reward design.}\nThese results establish that $\\dsyc$ reliably separates sycophantic from non-sycophantic representations across models, evidence-strength levels, and training checkpoints.\nDirection quality is not the bottleneck.\nThe reward failures documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap} therefore cannot be attributed to a noisy or degenerate direction; they point to a deeper incompatibility between discriminability and reward utility.\n","numLines":59,"startLine":1,"totalLines":59}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Hc1qRyfSYTKqFdvU11Nmf5","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Cosine \\texorpdfstring{$\\rone$}{R1}: Structural Failure}\n2\t\\label{sec:cosine_failure}\n3\t\n4\tSection~\\ref{sec:direction_quality} established that $\\dsyc$ achieves AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B using full-length responses.\n5\tDuring GRPO training, 256-token truncation reduces $\\dsyc$ AUROC to approximately 0.60, meaning the optimizer works with a substantially weaker signal than offline metrics suggest.\n6\tWe now test whether this discriminability translates into a viable GRPO reward.\n7\tFour configurations form a diagnostic chain (Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_chain}): each resolves the failure mode of its predecessor but exposes a deeper limitation.\n8\t\n9\t\\begin{table}[t]\n10\t\\centering\n11\t\\caption{Cosine $\\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$).}\n12\t\\label{tab:cosine_chain}\n13\t\\begin{tabular}{lllll}\n14\t\\toprule\n15\tConfig & $\\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\\\\n16\t\\midrule\n17\tF1a & $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h}\\cdot\\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\\\\n18\tF1b & $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\\rone \\approx 0$ & Gap too small \\\\\n19\tF1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\\\\n20\tF1d & $5\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\\rthree$ saturation \\\\\n21\t\\bottomrule\n22\t\\end{tabular}\n23\t\\end{table}\n24\t\n25\tF1a uses a dot-product formulation ($\\rone = \\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$), but residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\\times}$, saturating the tanh gate and zeroing the gradient by step 955.\n26\tSwitching to cosine similarity (F1b) eliminates norm dependence, yet the cosine gap between valid and sycophantic activations averages only 0.098 across layers---too narrow to generate meaningful reward contrast.\n27\tOnline recalibration (F1c, \\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}) raises offline AUROC from 0.827 to 0.910 but paradoxically triggers mode collapse: the abrupt shift in $\\dsyc$ at each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes GRPO advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.\n28\tAmplifying the signal with $5{\\times}$ scaling (F1d) lifts $\\rone$ above the noise floor but simultaneously amplifies the conflation between warranted revision and unwarranted capitulation; $\\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\\rone$ as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.\n29\t\n30\tTruncation further degrades the signal: under the 256-token maximum completion length used in GRPO, $\\dsyc$ AUROC drops from 0.76 (full response) to 0.60, so the offline discriminability (0.827) substantially overestimates the signal available during training.\n31\tCheckpoint-level evaluation confirms immediate collapse: $\\tof{=}0$ from step 500 onward with no stable phase.\n32\tThe four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \\emph{warranted}.\n33\tValid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.\n34\tThis is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\\dsyc$-based reward.\n35\tSection~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.\n36\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96913cdb-deaa-4598-a56a-44022bee38d4","uuid":"289a16e9-0e60-42b2-b313-ffe6f9257da5","timestamp":"2026-05-23T19:20:58.263Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/cosine_failure.tex","content":"\\section{Cosine \\texorpdfstring{$\\rone$}{R1}: Structural Failure}\n\\label{sec:cosine_failure}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:direction_quality} established that $\\dsyc$ achieves AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B using full-length responses.\nDuring GRPO training, 256-token truncation reduces $\\dsyc$ AUROC to approximately 0.60, meaning the optimizer works with a substantially weaker signal than offline metrics suggest.\nWe now test whether this discriminability translates into a viable GRPO reward.\nFour configurations form a diagnostic chain (Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_chain}): each resolves the failure mode of its predecessor but exposes a deeper limitation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine $\\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$).}\n\\label{tab:cosine_chain}\n\\begin{tabular}{lllll}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $\\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\\\\n\\midrule\nF1a & $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h}\\cdot\\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\\\\nF1b & $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\\rone \\approx 0$ & Gap too small \\\\\nF1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\\\\nF1d & $5\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\\rthree$ saturation \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nF1a uses a dot-product formulation ($\\rone = \\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$), but residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\\times}$, saturating the tanh gate and zeroing the gradient by step 955.\nSwitching to cosine similarity (F1b) eliminates norm dependence, yet the cosine gap between valid and sycophantic activations averages only 0.098 across layers---too narrow to generate meaningful reward contrast.\nOnline recalibration (F1c, \\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}) raises offline AUROC from 0.827 to 0.910 but paradoxically triggers mode collapse: the abrupt shift in $\\dsyc$ at each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes GRPO advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.\nAmplifying the signal with $5{\\times}$ scaling (F1d) lifts $\\rone$ above the noise floor but simultaneously amplifies the conflation between warranted revision and unwarranted capitulation; $\\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\\rone$ as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.\n\nTruncation further degrades the signal: under the 256-token maximum completion length used in GRPO, $\\dsyc$ AUROC drops from 0.76 (full response) to 0.60, so the offline discriminability (0.827) substantially overestimates the signal available during training.\nCheckpoint-level evaluation confirms immediate collapse: $\\tof{=}0$ from step 500 onward with no stable phase.\nThe four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \\emph{warranted}.\nValid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.\nThis is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\\dsyc$-based reward.\nSection~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.\n","numLines":36,"startLine":1,"totalLines":36}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01LCYs8uLMri21dfVt8QCHSb","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n2\t\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n3\t\n4\tThe cosine formulations fail because the continuous projection of $\\dsyc$ produces a gap too narrow for reward contrast.\n5\tA natural remedy is to discretize: train a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and threshold its output, yielding $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\n6\tWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\n7\tBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n8\t\n9\t\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\n10\tWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\n11\tThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n12\t\n13\t\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\n14\tWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\n15\tBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\n16\tQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n17\t\n18\t\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n19\t\n20\t\\begin{table}[t]\n21\t\\centering\n22\t\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n23\t\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n24\t\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n25\t\\toprule\n26\tStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n27\t\\midrule\n28\t500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n29\t1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n30\t1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n31\t2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n32\t\\bottomrule\n33\t\\end{tabular}\n34\t\\end{table}\n35\t\n36\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n37\t\\centering\n38\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n39\t\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n40\t\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n41\t\\end{figure}\n42\t\n43\tTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\n44\tAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\n45\tEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\n46\tThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\n47\tDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n48\t\n49\t\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\n50\tThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\n51\tWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\n52\tAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\n53\tEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n54\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96913cdb-deaa-4598-a56a-44022bee38d4","uuid":"d172c3b7-6a13-4cb5-94c1-ce51e0517948","timestamp":"2026-05-23T19:20:58.561Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/probe_trap.tex","content":"\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail because the continuous projection of $\\dsyc$ produces a gap too narrow for reward contrast.\nA natural remedy is to discretize: train a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and threshold its output, yielding $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000---well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy---and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$---continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen---will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n","numLines":54,"startLine":1,"totalLines":54}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0144ZwFBVMc87p5KTEzBQHxj","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{When Does Discriminability Fail as Reward Utility?}\\label{sec:discussion}\n2\t\n3\t\\paragraph{Root cause of the probe reward trap.}\n4\tThe probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) and the cosine failures (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) share a common geometric root.\n5\t$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\n6\tThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\n7\tCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\n8\tThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\n9\tThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\n10\tCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\n11\tThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n12\t\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable.\n13\tA quantitative comparison of geometric separability between $\\dsyc$ and their reward-hacking directions remains an important open question.\n14\tFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n15\t\n16\t\\paragraph{GRPO results under our configuration.}\n17\t\n18\t\\begin{table}[t]\n19\t\\centering\n20\t\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Best checkpoint (seed 1, step 6500); only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 2 (15 checkpoints); all exceed vanilla. Best $\\sycon{=}0.286$ (step 7000). $^\\S$All cosine variants collapsed from step 1 ($\\tof{=}0$ at every checkpoint); F1d additionally diverged at step 2160 (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$). $^\\P$ACT and ActAdd differ from GRPO along 5+ dimensions (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); included for context, not as controlled comparisons.}\n21\t\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n22\t\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n23\t\\toprule\n24\tMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n25\t\\midrule\n26\tVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n27\t\\midrule\n28\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n29\tCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\\\\n30\tContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n31\t\\midrule\n32\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{SFT with $\\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\\\\n33\tBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\n34\t\\midrule\n35\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n36\t$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\\\\n37\t\\midrule\n38\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\\P$}} \\\\\n39\tActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n40\t\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\n41\tACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\\\\n42\t\\bottomrule\n43\t\\end{tabular}\n44\t\\end{table}\n45\t\n46\tTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n47\t$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\n48\tIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\n49\tIt does not.\n50\tThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\n51\tSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000, its only checkpoint exceeding vanilla) and ends at 0.124 (checkpoint 8000).\n52\tOnly 4 of 28 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\\%): 3/12 for seed 1 (25\\%) and 1/16 for seed 2 (6.3\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\n53\tBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) with crash onset differing by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\n54\tActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: $\\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\\rthree$ saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by 17\\%, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\n55\tUnder our configuration (group size$=$2), $\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits a similar stubbornness pattern to the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity.\n56\t\n57\t\\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}\n58\tThree explanations are consistent with our GRPO results: (a)~$\\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a reward signal for sycophancy calibration; (b)~GRPO with group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity for any reward to achieve stable calibration; (c)~sycophancy calibration is inherently unsuitable for scalar per-completion RL.\n59\tTwo ablations address these hypotheses (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\n60\tIncreasing group size from 2 to 8 does not improve $\\rthree$-only GRPO: three checkpoints showed a monotonically declining trajectory ($0.084 \\to 0.085 \\to 0.054$), all below vanilla, and gs$=$8 (best 0.085) underperforms gs$=$2 (best 0.153); the experiment was terminated early.\n61\tThis rules out hypothesis~(b) as the primary explanation.\n62\tReducing ACT's LoRA configuration to match GRPO's (rank 64, $\\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) yields $\\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326 at step 7000); all 16 checkpoints exceed vanilla.\n63\tUnder identical LoRA configurations, per-token SFT ($\\sycon{=}0.267$) exceeds per-completion GRPO ($\\sycon{=}0.153$) by $1.7{\\times}$; supervision granularity is the primary driver, while LoRA capacity modulates convergence rate but is not the determining factor.\n64\tThe probe reward trap (C2) provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT where group size is irrelevant.\n65\t\n66\t\\begin{table}[t]\n67\t\\centering\n68\t\\small\n69\t\\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed monotonically declining trajectory. $^\\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\\sycon{=}0.267$.}\n70\t\\label{tab:ablation}\n71\t\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n72\t\\toprule\n73\tMethod & LoRA config & Best $\\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\\\\n74\t\\midrule\n75\tGRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\\\\n76\tGRPO gs$=$8$^\\dagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\\\\n77\t\\midrule\n78\tACT reduced-LoRA$^\\ddagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\\\\n79\tACT full s1 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\\\\n80\tACT full s2 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\\\\n81\t\\bottomrule\n82\t\\end{tabular}\n83\t\\end{table}\n84\t\n85\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n86\t\\centering\n87\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n88\t\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (two seeds) over training steps. ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 checkpoints (14.3\\%).}\n89\t\\label{fig:trajectory}\n90\t\\end{figure}\n91\t\n92\t\\paragraph{ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\n93\tACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ (seed 1) and $0.286$ (seed 2) at their respective peaks, more than $2{\\times}$ vanilla and above every GRPO variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}) (held-out validation confirms generalization; see Limitations).\n94\tThe trajectory is not monotonic---seed 1 dips to 0.214 at step 4000 but self-recovers by step 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists.\n95\tFull ACT peaks around steps 7000--8000 while reduced-LoRA surges to 0.326 at step 7000; both variants maintain above-vanilla performance throughout.\n96\tThe gap is categorical: ACT's worst checkpoint ($\\sycon{=}0.170$, full config) exceeds GRPO's absolute best ($\\sycon{=}0.153$, R3-only seed 1, step 6500).\n97\tThe reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) partially disentangles the five confounding dimensions: with LoRA capacity controlled, per-token SFT still dominates---and reduced-LoRA ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.301$), ruling out adapter capacity as the explanation.\n98\t\n99\t\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n100\t\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\n101\tTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n102\t\n103\t\\paragraph{Future directions.}\n104\tCondition-aware rewards, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and per-step process rewards offer paths forward; whether other REINFORCE-style methods or behavioral attributes with similar geometric entanglement~\\citep{skalse2022hacking} exhibit the same failures remains open.\n105\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96913cdb-deaa-4598-a56a-44022bee38d4","uuid":"aa27ed13-1dce-4d55-9098-0c65e3b58036","timestamp":"2026-05-23T19:21:04.848Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex","content":"\\section{When Does Discriminability Fail as Reward Utility?}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Root cause of the probe reward trap.}\nThe probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) and the cosine failures (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) share a common geometric root.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\nThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\nCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\nThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\nThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\nCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\nThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable.\nA quantitative comparison of geometric separability between $\\dsyc$ and their reward-hacking directions remains an important open question.\nFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO results under our configuration.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Best checkpoint (seed 1, step 6500); only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 2 (15 checkpoints); all exceed vanilla. Best $\\sycon{=}0.286$ (step 7000). $^\\S$All cosine variants collapsed from step 1 ($\\tof{=}0$ at every checkpoint); F1d additionally diverged at step 2160 (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$). $^\\P$ACT and ActAdd differ from GRPO along 5+ dimensions (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); included for context, not as controlled comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{SFT with $\\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\\P$}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_comparison} summarizes all methods evaluated on Qwen3-8B.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO removes $\\dsyc$ entirely and relies on the NLI-based behavioral reward alone.\nIf the failures in Sections~\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} and~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} stemmed solely from the representation reward, this ablation should recover calibration quality.\nIt does not.\nThe $\\sycon$ trajectory for $\\rthree$-only GRPO oscillates without sustained improvement.\nSeed 1 peaks at 0.153 (checkpoint 6500) and ends at 0.143 (checkpoint 8000); seed 2 peaks at 0.143 (checkpoint 4000, its only checkpoint exceeding vanilla) and ends at 0.124 (checkpoint 8000).\nOnly 4 of 28 checkpoints across both seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\\%): 3/12 for seed 1 (25\\%) and 1/16 for seed 2 (6.3\\%), while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nBoth seeds exhibit oscillations of similar amplitude ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) with crash onset differing by ${\\sim}2{,}500$ steps, indicating stochastic drift in a saturated reward landscape.\nActivation analysis confirms the drift is not representational: $\\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\\rthree$ saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by 17\\%, reinforcing the disconnect between discriminability and behavioral utility.\nUnder our configuration (group size$=$2), $\\rthree$-only GRPO exhibits a similar stubbornness pattern to the $\\dsyc$ approaches (high $\\nof$, near-zero $\\tof$) at milder severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}\nThree explanations are consistent with our GRPO results: (a)~$\\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a reward signal for sycophancy calibration; (b)~GRPO with group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity for any reward to achieve stable calibration; (c)~sycophancy calibration is inherently unsuitable for scalar per-completion RL.\nTwo ablations address these hypotheses (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\nIncreasing group size from 2 to 8 does not improve $\\rthree$-only GRPO: three checkpoints showed a monotonically declining trajectory ($0.084 \\to 0.085 \\to 0.054$), all below vanilla, and gs$=$8 (best 0.085) underperforms gs$=$2 (best 0.153); the experiment was terminated early.\nThis rules out hypothesis~(b) as the primary explanation.\nReducing ACT's LoRA configuration to match GRPO's (rank 64, $\\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) yields $\\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326 at step 7000); all 16 checkpoints exceed vanilla.\nUnder identical LoRA configurations, per-token SFT ($\\sycon{=}0.267$) exceeds per-completion GRPO ($\\sycon{=}0.153$) by $1.7{\\times}$; supervision granularity is the primary driver, while LoRA capacity modulates convergence rate but is not the determining factor.\nThe probe reward trap (C2) provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT where group size is irrelevant.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed monotonically declining trajectory. $^\\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\\sycon{=}0.267$.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & LoRA config & Best $\\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\\\\n\\midrule\nGRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\\\\nGRPO gs$=$8$^\\dagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nACT reduced-LoRA$^\\ddagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\\\\nACT full s1 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\\\\nACT full s2 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (two seeds) over training steps. ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 checkpoints (14.3\\%).}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT~\\citep{consistency2025bctact} achieves $\\sycon = 0.301$ (seed 1) and $0.286$ (seed 2) at their respective peaks, more than $2{\\times}$ vanilla and above every GRPO variant (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}) (held-out validation confirms generalization; see Limitations).\nThe trajectory is not monotonic---seed 1 dips to 0.214 at step 4000 but self-recovers by step 6000, unlike GRPO where stubbornness persists.\nFull ACT peaks around steps 7000--8000 while reduced-LoRA surges to 0.326 at step 7000; both variants maintain above-vanilla performance throughout.\nThe gap is categorical: ACT's worst checkpoint ($\\sycon{=}0.170$, full config) exceeds GRPO's absolute best ($\\sycon{=}0.153$, R3-only seed 1, step 6500).\nThe reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) partially disentangles the five confounding dimensions: with LoRA capacity controlled, per-token SFT still dominates---and reduced-LoRA ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.301$), ruling out adapter capacity as the explanation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), consistent with per-completion information compression as an independent factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and per-step process rewards offer paths forward; whether other REINFORCE-style methods or behavioral attributes with similar geometric entanglement~\\citep{skalse2022hacking} exhibit the same failures remains open.\n","numLines":105,"startLine":1,"totalLines":105}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01PoExJZYoVTjUPWFdBQ1k3A","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Introduction}\n2\t\\label{sec:introduction}\n3\t\n4\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n5\t\\centering\n6\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}\n7\t\\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}\n8\t\\label{fig:teaser}\n9\t\\end{figure}\n10\t\n11\tRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\n12\tA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\n13\tThese results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.\n14\tSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n15\t\n16\tThis paper documents the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n17\tIn our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping---the most common safeguard against reward hacking \\citep{casper2023openproblems}---would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.\n18\tBecause the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.\n19\t\n20\tUnder our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), two additional $\\dsyc$-based formulations---cosine projection (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) and continuous probe---also produced behavioral collapse.\n21\tRemoving $\\dsyc$ entirely, $\\rthree$-only GRPO (behavioral reward alone) exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%) across two seeds and a group-size ablation.\n22\tACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact} (dense per-token SFT) exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), and a LoRA-controlled ablation confirms the reliability gap reflects supervision granularity.\n23\t\n24\tThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\n25\tTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.\n26\tFor reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.\n27\tSycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$ \\citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.\n28\tWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n29\t\n30\tThis paper presents a negative result with mechanistic analysis.\n31\tOur two principal findings (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}):\n32\t\n33\t\\begin{itemize}\n34\t\\item \\textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n35\t\n36\t\\item \\textbf{Categorical reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 (12.9\\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\\times}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n37\t\\end{itemize}\n38\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96913cdb-deaa-4598-a56a-44022bee38d4","uuid":"daad0de4-6f82-456e-b764-0ec17c145255","timestamp":"2026-05-23T19:21:05.258Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex","content":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}\n\\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nThese results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper documents the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nIn our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping---the most common safeguard against reward hacking \\citep{casper2023openproblems}---would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.\nBecause the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.\n\nUnder our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), two additional $\\dsyc$-based formulations---cosine projection (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) and continuous probe---also produced behavioral collapse.\nRemoving $\\dsyc$ entirely, $\\rthree$-only GRPO (behavioral reward alone) exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%) across two seeds and a group-size ablation.\nACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact} (dense per-token SFT) exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), and a LoRA-controlled ablation confirms the reliability gap reflects supervision granularity.\n\nThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.\nSycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$ \\citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n\nThis paper presents a negative result with mechanistic analysis.\nOur two principal findings (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}):\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Categorical reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 (12.9\\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\\times}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n","numLines":38,"startLine":1,"totalLines":38}}}
